]
during the study of the quantum equation of motion.

Comment 3.
The counter-vertex
S_{\mathrm{f}}[a,B]
with a logarithmic coefficient
L
has a standard form and is completely consistent with the known results, see formula (3.42) in
[
105
]
. An interesting fact is that it is introduced out of the need to reduce ”nonlocal” singularities in the quantum action. For example, in
[
120
]
this vertex was determined differently, from the renormalization of the quantum equation of motion. Thus, at the level of the first two corrections, it can be seen that the quantum action on the diagonal is closed from the point of view of determining the renormalization constants.

Comment 4.
The counter-term
S_{2}[B]
and the counter-term
S_{2}[a]
are included together with power-law singularities
\Lambda^{2}
. Such terms are standard in the case of cutoff and symbolize the loss of invariance with respect to the gauge transformations of the background field. The coefficients depend on the regularizing function
\mathbf{f}(\cdot)
.

Comment 5.
Note that in the theorem, all coefficients with logarithmic singularities are provided with additional free constants, which can be fixed based on the choice of the subtraction scheme. At the same time, terms with power singularities do not have such additions. This choice is not important and is used by the authors solely for the convenience of calculations in the next corrections. If necessary, we can add free constants.

Proof.
Following the formulation, we divide the solution of the equations into three parts.

The first part.
The equality of the singular part to zero for the first quantum renormalized correction (
78
) boils down to searching for singularities for ”
\ln\det
” and further defining the coefficient
a_{0}
and the auxiliary functional
\hat{W}_{1}^{0}
so that the result is finite. In Section
6
, the determinants are investigated using explicit perturbative formulas (
98
) and (
134
). The results consist of two parts:

\displaystyle\ln\det(G_{0}|_{\mathrm{reg.}})\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\mbox{see formula}\,\,\eqref{ya-a-64};

\displaystyle\ln\det(G_{1}|_{\mathrm{reg.}})\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\mbox{see formula}\,\,\eqref{ya-a-69}.

Substituting the results into (
78
) and equating the singular part to zero, we obtain an equation of the form

-\frac{c_{2}\Lambda^{2}(\rho_{3}-2\rho_{0})}{8\pi^{2}}S_{2}[B]-\frac{11c_{2}L}{96\pi^{2}}W_{-1}[B]+\frac{1}{4}\Big(a_{0}W_{-1}^{\phantom{1}}[B]+\hat{W}_{1}^{0}[B]\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}0,

where
L=\ln(\Lambda/\sigma)
. By solving this linear equation, we obtain the answers indicated in the formulation.

The second part.
The equality of the singular part to zero for the first variation in the field responsible for the gauge condition is reduced to the variation of the determinants, which, in turn, are reduced to the calculation of one-loop contributions
\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(V_{1}\big)
and
\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(V_{2}\big)
. They are studied in Section
6
, and the results are presented in formulas:

\displaystyle-\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(V_{1}\big)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,e_{\mu}^{g}(z)\Bigg(\frac{\delta}{\delta e_{\mu}^{g}(z)}\ln\det(\mathfrak{G}_{0}|_{\mathrm{reg.}})\bigg|_{e=B}\Bigg)\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\mbox{see formula}\,\,\eqref{ya-a-67};

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(V_{2}\big)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,e_{\mu}^{g}(z)\Bigg(\frac{\delta}{\delta e_{\mu}^{g}(z)}\ln\det(\mathfrak{G}_{1}|_{\mathrm{reg.}})\bigg|_{e=B}\Bigg)\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\mbox{see formula}\,\,\eqref{ya-a-73}.

Substituting the obtained relations into (
80
) and equating the singular part to zero, we obtain an equation of the form

\frac{1}{4}\hat{W}_{1}^{1}[B,\varepsilon]-\frac{c_{2}L}{8\pi^{2}}\Gamma_{1}[\varepsilon]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}0

from which follows the stated answer.

The third part.
Consider the second correction for the action on the diagonal. In this case, it is convenient to study the linear combination of standard diagrams separately:

-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{3}^{2}\big)+\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{4}^{\phantom{1}}\big)-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Omega_{3}^{2}\big)\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\mbox{see formula}\,\,\eqref{ya-a-166}.

Additionally, we substitute the relations of (
215
) and (
216
) for nonlocal parts into the resulting ratio, using the definitions for the counter-vertices

\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(S_{2}[\,\cdot\,]\big)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,G_{1\rho\rho}^{\,\,aa}(x,x),\,\,\,\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(S_{\mathrm{f}}[\,\cdot\,,B]\big)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big(D_{\rho}^{bc}(x)D_{\sigma}^{ca}(x)G_{1\sigma\rho}^{\,\,ab}(x,y)\Big)\Big|_{y=x}.

(86)

Then, after substituting and cancelling some terms, we obtain the equation of the form

-\frac{\Lambda^{2}c_{2}(2\rho_{3}-3\rho_{0})}{8\pi^{2}}\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(S_{2}[\,\cdot\,]\big)+\frac{5Lc_{2}}{48\pi^{2}}\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(S_{\mathrm{f}}[\,\cdot\,,B]\big)+\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\hat{\Gamma}_{2}^{0}\big)\\
-\frac{c_{2}^{2}W_{-1}L}{32(4\pi^{2})^{2}}\Big(13+68\rho_{2}/3-64\rho_{4}\Big)+\frac{c_{2}^{2}W_{-1}^{+}L}{32(4\pi^{2})^{2}}\Big(2\rho_{2}-1/2\Big)\\
+2\mathrm{J}_{\ominus}+\frac{c_{2}\rho_{3}\Lambda^{2}\mathrm{J}_{\odot}}{\pi^{2}}-\frac{5Lc_{2}}{48\pi^{2}}\mathrm{J}_{\otimes}+\frac{1}{4}\Big(a_{1}W_{-1}^{\phantom{1}}+\hat{W}_{2}^{0}\Big)+\kappa_{1}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}0.

(87)

If the singular part is equal to zero, the answer for the vertex
\hat{\Gamma}_{2}^{0}[B,a]
immediately follows, so the first line can be excluded from the ratio. Further, the second line has a suitable form as the sum of the classical action and one of its special parts, see (
226
). Therefore, next we need to use calculations for the remaining diagrams:

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{\otimes}\,\,

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\,\,\mbox{see formula}\,\,\eqref{ya-a-188};

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{\odot}\,\,

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\,\,\mbox{see formula}\,\,\eqref{ya-a-190};

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{\ominus}\,\,

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\,\,\mbox{see formula}\,\,\eqref{ya-a-191}.

Summing up and substituting into (
87
), we get the stated answers for
a_{1}(\Lambda)
and
\hat{W}_{2}^{0}[B]
. The theorem has been proved.

5
Weak deformation

5.1
General remarks

In Section
4
, a variant of strong deformation was presented, which consisted of probabilistic averaging of fluctuation fields, see formula (
55
). One of the problems associated with this approach was the loss of invariance under the gauge transformations of the background field
B_{\mu}^{a}
. It is precisely because of this fact that at the level of the second quantum correction, see Theorem
1
in Section
4.7
, the singular contribution proportional to the classical action
W_{-1}
was split into several parts
W_{-1}^{i}
. This indicates a significant complication of the renormalization process, as it essentially restricts the possibilities of using ”simple”
40
40

40

This refers to the exclusion of terms from the expansions for the Green’s function, which obviously will not lead to singular components. For examples, see Section
8.1
.
decompositions for the Green’s functions, effectively reducing them to zero.

In this section, an alternative approach to the introduction of regularization is proposed, which consists in deforming the fluctuation fields by acting on them with a special gauge invariant operator depending on
B_{\mu}^{a}
. At the same time, in the main order, when decomposed into ”small” fields, such an operator reproduces the probabilistic averaging from Section
4.1
. Such a deformation will be called weak for two reasons. First of all, it does not lead
41
41

41

Hypothetically, the deformation can be chosen in such a way that the first loop is also regularized. That is, so that the increase in eigenvalues is ”compensated” by a decrease in the regularized spectral density. However, the authors did not manage to study this fact well enough.
to the regularization of the first loop, that is ”
\ln\det
”. For these purposes, one should use, for example, the regularization of the heat kernel, see Section 6.2 in
[
122
]
and section
\mathrm{B}
in
[
126
]
, or the analytical continuation of the
\zeta
-function
[
127
]
. A similar situation occurs in other regularizations, see
[
128
]
. Secondly, such regularization additionally deforms the relationship between the quantum action and the density of the quantum equation of motion, which leads to additional difficulties in recalculation.

Nevertheless, the weak deformation approach is very attractive from the point of view of multi-loop calculations. This is primarily due to the fact that the elementary blocks that make up the Feynman diagrams are invariant with respect to the gauge transformations of the background field
B_{\mu}^{a}
. The following important properties are an immediate consequence of this fact.

1.

Logarithmic local contributions are proportional only to
W_{-1}[B]
.

2.

There are no power-law local contributions.

Such constraints significantly simplify the calculation process and allow us to replace the decomposition for Green’s functions near the diagonal with simpler functions. This process is described in detail in Section
8
devoted to the study of ”two loops”.

5.2
Regularization

Let us discuss the process of introducing regularization. To do this, we use the formulations of the two methods for the case of strong deformation from Section
4.2
and adjust them. This is possible because, according to the construction, the ”strong” case should be reproduced in the main order for the case of weak (small) fields.

The first way.
Let us formulate the rules for the deformation of Green’s functions. To do this, as in Section
4.2
, we choose a piecewise continuous function
\omega(\cdot)
as the averaging kernel with the same properties
42
42

42

A generalization of the properties can be found in
[
63
,
61
]
.
. Next, note that the double averaging of the free Green’s function, described by the transition (
50
) using the integral operator and reducing to the averaging of the oscillating exponent, can be rewritten in operator form as follows
43
43

43

The right hand side of (
88
) must be understood in the sense of operators. That is, this is the Taylor series by degrees of the operator. Note that only even powers arise in the expansion.

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}y\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,\omega(|y|)\omega(|z|)\,e^{ik\cdot(x+y/\Lambda+z/\Lambda)}=\hat{\Omega}^{2}(r/\Lambda)\Big|_{r^{2}=A_{0}(x)}e^{ik\cdot x},

(88)

where
r=|x|
,
A_{0}(x)=-\partial_{x_{\mu}}\partial_{x^{\mu}}
, which in hyperspherical coordinates is equal to
-r^{-3}\partial_{r}r^{3}\partial_{r}
for the case of spherically symmetric functions, as well as an auxiliary function

\hat{\Omega}(r)=2S_{3}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\,t^{2}\omega(t)J_{1}(tr)/r.

(89)

Note that formula (20) from
[
60
]
was used for the last transition. Next, returning to the Green’s functions, we define deformations as follows

G_{0}^{\Lambda,\,ab}(x,y)=\bigg(\hat{\Omega}^{2}(r/\Lambda)\Big|_{r^{2}=M_{0}(x)}\bigg)^{ac}G_{0}^{cb}(x,y),

(90)

as well as

\displaystyle G_{1\sigma\rho}^{\Lambda\,ab}(x,y)=\delta_{\sigma\rho}G_{0}^{\Lambda,\,ab}(x,y)+\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}

\displaystyle 2^{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4\times k}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{k}\,G_{0}^{\Lambda,\,ad_{1}}(x,z_{1})\times

(91)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle F_{\sigma\mu_{1}}^{d_{1}c_{1}}(z_{1})G_{0}^{\Lambda,\,c_{1}d_{2}}(z_{1},z_{2})\times\ldots\times F_{\mu_{k}\rho}^{d_{k}c_{k}}(z_{k})G_{0}^{\Lambda,\,c_{k}b}(z_{k},y),

(92)

It follows from the construction that the functions change covariantly with respect to the gauge transformation (
26
), since they are constructed using the gauge invariant operator
M_{0}^{ab}(x)
, and their expansion near the diagonal for the special case of fields was previously studied in
[
60
]
, see Section 4.1.

The second way.
Additionally, we point out an alternative view of the introduction of operator (
90
). It is completely similar to the one described in Section
4.2
, so let us pay attention to only two key changes.

1.

The fluctuation and ghost fields are changed not by the averaging operator (
55
), but by the operator function (
89
). For example, deformed fluctuations have the form

a_{\mu}^{\Lambda,a}(x)=\bigg(\hat{\Omega}(r/\Lambda)\Big|_{r^{2}=M_{0}(x)}\bigg)^{ab}a_{\mu}^{b}(x).

(93)

The fields
c^{a},\,\bar{c}^{a}
are modified in the same way.

2.

The substitution from the first point applies to all fields except those in quadratic form with the operator
M_{0}
, that is, in

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,a_{\mu}^{a}(x)M_{0}^{ab}(x)a_{\mu}^{b}(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\bar{c}^{a}(x)M_{0}^{ab}(x)c^{b}(x).

By decomposing the action into a perturbative series, we can make sure that the last rules are equivalent to the transition to the deformed Green’s functions (
90
) and (
17
).

About the determinant.
As was already noted in Section
5.2
, the proposed approach with a covariant deforming operator does not guarantee the regularization of the first correction with ”
\ln\det
”. For these purposes, additional regularization should be used, which can be introduced either within the framework of the heat kernel method, for example, using an analytical continuation or a cutoff, or by simple division and multiplication (deformation of the measure of the functional integral). Since it is the singular parts that are studied in this paper, and the singularity in the first correction does not depend on regularization, then for further study it is enough to have only the relation

\ln\det\big(G_{0}/G_{0}|_{B=0}\big)\big|_{\mathrm{reg.}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}-\frac{c_{2}L}{96\pi^{2}}W_{-1}[B].

(94)

As expected, the contribution does not contain power-law parts
(\sim\Lambda)
, since the value is invariant with respect to the gauge transformations of the background field.

Expansion.
Next, we note that it is not necessary to use the background field
B_{\mu}^{a}
to introduce the last regularization, but another
r_{\mu}^{a}
can be selected, which changes under the gauge transformations in the same way as the background field. Of course, when calculating singular parts, it is the specific choice of
r_{\mu}^{a}=B_{\mu}^{a}
that is important for the quantum action. However, when trying to calculate the variation in the field from the deforming operator, it becomes necessary to distinguish the fields. For these purposes, for example, it is convenient to choose
r_{\mu}^{a}=B_{\mu}^{a}+\varepsilon_{\mu}^{a}
, then

\frac{\delta}{\delta r_{\mu}^{a}(x)}\bigg|_{r=B}=\frac{\delta}{\delta\varepsilon_{\mu}^{a}(x)}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}.

In this regard, we expand the notation for the regularized quantum action by adding a third argument, which is responsible for the field in the regularizing operator. Thus, the strongly connected part of (
24
), regularized by the above procedure with
B_{\mu}^{a}\to r_{\mu}^{a}
, is further notated by the symbol

W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{\mathrm{sc}}[B,e,r],\,\,\,\mbox{as well as}\,\,\,W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{\mathrm{sc}}[B]\equiv W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{\mathrm{sc}}[B,B,B].

(95)

5.3
Situation around diagonal

Problematic.
In the case of weak deformation, the variation of the quantum action on the diagonal with respect to the background field, see (
57
), becomes more complicated, since the deforming operator additionally depends on the auxiliary field
r_{\mu}^{a}(x)
. Thus, the first variation of the action on the diagonal, which in this case is characterized by the condition
r_{\mu}^{a}=e_{\mu}^{a}=B_{\mu}^{a}
, described by the formula

\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]}{\delta B_{\mu}^{a}(x)}=\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B,e,r]}{\delta B_{\mu}^{a}(x)}\Bigg|_{r=e=B}+\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B,e,B]}{\delta e_{\mu}^{a}(x)}\Bigg|_{e=B}+\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B,B,r]}{\delta r_{\mu}^{a}(x)}\Bigg|_{r=B},

(96)

in which only the first term is equal to the density of the regularized quantum equation of motion. Once again, note that since the action depends on the regularizing operator
\hat{\Omega}
, the inequality generally holds

\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B,e,B]}{\delta B_{\mu}^{a}(x)}\Bigg|_{e=B}\neq\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B,e,r]}{\delta B_{\mu}^{a}(x)}\Bigg|_{r=e=B}\equiv Q_{\mu}^{a}|_{\mathrm{reg.}}[B](x).

Thus, in order to recalculate the renormalization values from the quantum action to the density of the equation of motion, it is necessary to track not only the variation with respect to
e_{\mu}^{a}
, the singular part of which, in the case of strong deformation, determines the auxiliary current
j_{\mu}^{a}(x)
, see (
75
), but also a variation with respect to
r_{\mu}^{a}
. In this formulation, the task becomes significantly more complicated, so it is easier to calculate not both variations, but the singularities for the quantum equation directly. That is, in addition to renormalizing the quantum action, it is also necessary to study the renormalization of the object
Q_{\mu}^{a}|_{\mathrm{reg.}}[B](x)
in parallel.

Renormalization.
Taking into account the last remarks, we will clarify what exactly will be understood by the renormalization of the quantum action and the equation of motion. First, consider the fields
r_{\mu}^{a}
and
e_{\mu}^{a}
, equal
44
44

44

In the general case, there is no equality. This choice is convenient for further calculation.
to each other and close to the background field, that is

r_{\mu}^{a}(x)=e_{\mu}^{a}(x)=B_{\mu}^{a}(x)+\varepsilon_{\mu}^{a}.

Then the renormalization of the pair
W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]
and
Q_{\mu}^{a}|_{\mathrm{reg.}}[B]
consists in shifting the coupling constant
g\to g_{\Lambda}
, which is expressed as a series in terms of a renormalized constant

\frac{1}{g^{2}_{\Lambda}}=\frac{1}{g_{\mathrm{ren}}^{2}}+\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}a_{k}^{\phantom{1}}(\Lambda)g_{\mathrm{ren}}^{2k},

and adding a set of auxiliary terms to the classical action, as was done in the case of strong deformation, see (
61
). However, given the invariance with respect to the gauge transformations of the background field, the terms can be written out in a simpler form

\bigg(\sum_{k=2}^{+\infty}g^{k}\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{0}[B,a]\bigg)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\varepsilon_{\mu}^{a}(x)j_{\mu}^{a}(x)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

where the auxiliary current
j_{\mu}^{a}(x)
, taking into account the explicit form for the single vertex
\Gamma_{1}[a]
from (
8
), is determined by the equality

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\varepsilon_{\mu}^{a}(x)j_{\mu}^{a}(x)=\frac{\Gamma_{1}[\varepsilon]}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}g^{2k-2}w_{k}(\Lambda)+\sum_{k=2}^{+\infty}g^{k}\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{1}[B,a].

Let us pay attention to what values are found in this case after performing the renormalization:

\displaystyle W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\{a_{k},\hat{\Gamma}_{k+2}^{0}\}_{k=0}^{+\infty};

\displaystyle\{a_{k},\hat{\Gamma}_{k+2}^{0}\}_{k=0}^{+\infty}\,\,+\,\,Q_{\mu}^{a}|_{\mathrm{reg.}}[B](x)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\{w_{k},\hat{\Gamma}_{k+1}^{1}\}_{k=1}^{+\infty},

where, at the last step, a set of renormalization data is obtained from considering the difference between the two studied objects

\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B,B+\varepsilon,B+\varepsilon]}{\delta\varepsilon_{\mu}^{a}(x)}\Bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}=\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]}{\delta B_{\mu}^{a}(x)}-Q_{\mu}^{a}|_{\mathrm{reg.}}[B](x).

Moreover, the sequence is similar to the one in Section
4.5
, see formulas (
72
) and (
73
). The ansatz looks much simpler than in formula (
61
), since the regularization is introduced covariantly. It is for this reason that the functionals
\hat{W}_{k}^{1}
are explicitly written out as
w_{k}\Gamma_{1}[\varepsilon]
. Thus, it is necessary to look for the proportionality coefficient
w_{k}
, rather than the whole functional and its form.

5.4
What do we calculate?

Let us introduce the expansion coefficients for the renormalized quantum action on the diagonal and its first variation on the second argument, also on the diagonal. Formally, they are the same as in formulas (
76
) and (
77
), so for convenience we will use the same notation. To clarify, in the ”weak” case there is also a third argument, it is chosen as
r_{\mu}^{a}=B_{\mu}^{a}
. The coefficients themselves have the same form
45
45

45

Note that the operator
\mathbb{H}
connects vertices with the regularized Green’s functions. In the ”strong” and ”weak” cases, they are different, see for comparison (
53
) and (
90
).
, as in (
78
), (
79
), and (
80
).

The main task of this section is to renormalize the quantum action on the diagonal, taking into account the first two corrections, as well as to demonstrate the fact that the variation with respect to the deforming operator within the framework of the first quantum correction does not contain singular parts. Thus, it is planned to calculate the following values:

\displaystyle\mbox{1-st correction for}\,\,W_{\mathrm{ren}}^{\mathrm{sc}}

\displaystyle\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\mbox{answer for}\,\,a_{0};

\displaystyle\mbox{1-st correction for}\,\,\dot{W}_{\mathrm{ren}}^{\mathrm{sc}}

\displaystyle\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\mbox{answer for}\,\,w_{1};

\displaystyle\mbox{2-nd correction for}\,\,W_{\mathrm{ren}}^{\mathrm{sc}}

\displaystyle\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\mbox{answers for}\,\,\hat{\Gamma}_{2}^{0}\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,a_{1}.

And also additionally prove equality in the sense of formal relations by the coupling constant

\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]}{\delta B_{\mu}^{a}(x)}-Q_{\mu}^{a}|_{\mathrm{reg.}}[B](x)-\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B,e,B]}{\delta e_{\mu}^{a}(x)}\Bigg|_{e=B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}\mathcal{O}(g^{2}).

(97)

5.5
Results

Theorem 2
.

Let us assume that all the assumptions formulated above in Section
4
are fulfilled. Let also
L=\ln(\Lambda/\sigma)
, where
\Lambda
is a regularization parameter and
\sigma>0
is an auxiliary fixed parameter to make combinations dimensionless. We additionally define the functionals

S_{2}[B]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,B_{\mu}^{a}(x)B_{\mu}^{a}(x),\,\,\,S_{\mathrm{f}}[a,e]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big(D_{\mu}^{ab}(x)a_{\mu}^{b}(x)\Big)\Big(\mathfrak{D}_{\nu}^{ac}(x)a_{\nu}^{c}(x)\Big),

and several auxiliary numbers depending on the regularized free fundamental solution
R_{0}^{1}(x)
, see (
51
),

\frac{\rho_{0}}{4\pi^{2}}=R_{0}^{1}(0),\,\,\,\frac{\rho_{3}}{4\pi^{2}}=\int_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,R_{0}^{1}(x)A_{0}^{\phantom{1}}(x)R_{0}^{1}(x),\,\,\,\frac{2\rho_{4}}{\pi^{2}}=\int_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,R_{0}^{1}(x)|x|^{2}A_{0}^{\phantom{1}}(x)R_{0}^{1}(x),

\frac{\rho_{2}}{8\pi^{2}}=\int_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,A_{0}^{\phantom{1}}(x)R_{0}^{1}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}y\,\Big(R_{0}^{1}(x-y)R_{0}^{1}(y)-R_{0}^{1}(y)R_{0}^{1}(y)\Big).

Let also the numbers
\tilde{a}
and
\{\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{b}_{i}\}_{i=0,1}
denote arbitrary finite elements from
\mathbb{R}
. Then the presented equalities (
84
) and (
85
) lead to the following coefficients and auxiliary vertices

1)\,\,W_{0}^{\Lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}0\,\,\Longrightarrow\,\,a_{0}(\Lambda)=\frac{11c_{2}L}{24\pi^{2}}+\tilde{a}_{0}.

2)\,\,\dot{W}_{0}^{\Lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}0\,\,\Longrightarrow\,\,\omega_{1}(\Lambda)=\frac{c_{2}L}{2\pi^{2}}+\tilde{b}_{0}.

\displaystyle 3)\,\,W_{1}^{\Lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}0\,\,\Longrightarrow\,\,\hat{\Gamma}_{2}^{0}[B]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda^{2}c_{2}(2\rho_{3}-3\rho_{0})}{8\pi^{2}}S_{2}[a]-\bigg(\frac{5Lc_{2}}{48\pi^{2}}+\tilde{b}_{1}\bigg)S_{\mathrm{f}}[a,B],

\displaystyle a_{1}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{2}^{2}L}{(4\pi)^{4}}\Big(175/9+2^{4}7\rho_{2}/3-2^{7}\rho_{4}\Big)+\tilde{a}_{1}.

Comment 1.
As in the case of the result from Theorem
1
, the first coefficient
a_{0}
has the standard value
46
46

46

See the links in the comments to Theorem
1
.
. The second coefficient
a_{1}
has a different value. In Section
8.5
, a detailed comparison is made with the case of dimensional regularization. It was shown that the answer for the coefficient is constructed from a finite set of master-integrals. They are listed in Table
2
. At the same time, Table
3
shows the results for individual diagrams and their linear combinations. It turned out that in the case of dimensional regularization, the coefficients with which the master-integrals enter are deformed, while in the case of cutoff, the integrals themselves are deformed. This observation is complemented by the fact that, in the case of cutoff, the singularity for each master-integral can be divided into the main part, which is transferred to the dimensional case, and the auxiliary correction part, which depends on the regularizing function.

Comment 2.
The coefficient
w_{1}
has the standard form, a more detailed comparison is made in a similar comment for Theorem
1
.

Comment 3.
The counter-vertex
S_{\mathrm{f}}[a,B]
also has the standard form and is discussed in a similar comment for Theorem
1
.

Comment 4.
Note that the counter-term
S_{2}[B]
with power singularity
\Lambda^{2}
is missing, which is a consequence of the covariance of regularized objects. However, the counter-vertex
S_{2}[a]
remains and has the same form as in Theorem
1
. Such a term does not violate gauge invariance, since the field
a_{\mu}^{a}
transforms according to a different law, see (
28
).

Proof.
In many ways, the calculation repeats the process of verifying the statements of Theorem
1
, so we give only auxiliary comments. Following the above description, we divide the explanation into three parts.

The first part.
The calculation of the singular part for the ”first loop” is performed in two stages. First, it is necessary to take advantage of the fact that the singular contribution for the determinant of the scalar operator
M_{0}^{ab}
under conditions of weak deformation is given by equation (
94
). Further, following the logic of counting for the ”strong” case, it is convenient to represent the singular part for a vector operator as the sum of the part depending on the scalar operator and the contribution consisting of potentials, see (
135
). Considering the fact that, in the main order, the Green’s functions for both regularizations coincide in construction, see (
53
) and (
90
), then the answer is dictated by formula (
137
) without a power singularity with
S_{2}[B]
. Considering the linear combination on the right hand side of (
78
), we get the stated answer.

The second part.
In this case, it is necessary to calculate the singular part for the diagrams
-\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(V_{1})
and
-\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(V_{2})
, see the proof of Theorem
1
and the definition (
80
). In this case, an approach with direct calculation of variations for determinants, as was done in Section
6
, will not work, since the deforming operator also depends on the background field. Therefore, at first glance, an explicit calculation of both diagrams is necessary. However, note that the singular part for the second diagram, after using the expansion near the diagonal in the form (
259
) and the definitions (
66
) and (
67
), is written out as

\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(V_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}-\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(V_{1})-c_{2}\theta(0)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\varepsilon_{\mu}^{a}(x)D_{\nu}^{ab}(x)F_{\nu\mu}^{b}(x),

where notation (
149
) was used for the special function
\theta(\cdot)
. Therefore, the combination of interest from (
80
), taking into account (
240
), is rewritten as follows

-\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(V_{1})-\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(V_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}-\frac{c_{2}L}{8\pi^{2}}\Gamma_{1}[\varepsilon]=-\frac{1}{4}\hat{W}_{1}^{1}[B,\varepsilon].

Further, taking into account equality
\hat{W}_{1}^{1}[B,\varepsilon]=w_{1}\Gamma_{1}[\varepsilon]
, we get the stated answer.

The third part.
In this case, the proof process is almost identical to the third point of the proof of Theorem
1
. The main difference lies in the decomposition of the Green’s functions near the diagonal, see Section
8.1
. This leads to the need to transform a number of small calculations, which is described in Section
8.2
, after which the linear combination of the main diagrams takes the form (
265
). An additional counter-diagram is given in Section
8.5
, while the final answers for the local part and counter-diagrams are written out in Tables
1
and
3
. The theorem has been proved.

5.6
But what if so?

From a computational point of view, the consistency of renormalization of the quantum action with renormalization of the density from the quantum equation of motion, see Sections
4.5
and
5.3
, significantly complicates the entire process. Indeed, it is actually necessary to renormalize not one line, but two. And if we keep consistency with a large number of variational derivatives of the action, then the task becomes even more difficult. In this regard, there is a desire to simplify the renormalization algorithm, limiting itself to working only with the quantum action. But what happens in this case? Let us briefly describe the sequence of actions. We will consider the quantum action directly on the diagonal, that is

W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]\equiv W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B,B,B]\,\,\,\mbox{Instead of}\,\,\,W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B,e,r].

Next, we replace the standard strongly connected
47
47

47

This fact is verified using functional relations from Section
4.2
.
regularized Green’s functions
48
48

48

It is worth noting here that we are talking about functions with cutted external lines off (or ”imputed legs”).

\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{k}}^{a_{1}\ldots a_{k}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{\delta B_{\mu_{k}}^{a_{k}}(x_{k})}\cdot\ldots\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta B_{\mu_{1}}^{a_{1}}(x_{1})}W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B,e,r]\bigg)\Bigg|_{r=e=B},

where
k\in\mathbb{N}
, with deformed analogues of the form

\hat{\Gamma}_{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{k}}^{a_{1}\ldots a_{k}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=\frac{\delta}{\delta B_{\mu_{k}}^{a_{k}}(x_{k})}\cdot\ldots\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta B_{\mu_{1}}^{a_{1}}(x_{1})}W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B].

For example, in this case, the density of the quantum equation of motion is replaced by the total derivative

Q_{\mu}^{a}|_{\mathrm{reg.}}[B](x)=\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B,e,r]}{\delta B_{\mu}^{a}(x)}\Bigg|_{r=e=B}\longrightarrow\,\,\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]}{\delta B_{\mu}^{a}(x)}.

Next, let us pay attention to the fact that the connected quantum action can be constructed as a sum in which the first term is the strongly connected action, and the next parts are constructed by density from the quantum equation and tree diagrams, at the nodes of which strongly connected Green’s functions sit, see for example (
32
). Thus,
W^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]
is the functional that depends on the set
\{\Gamma_{\ldots}^{\ldots}\}
. Let us define the deformed connected action
\hat{W}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]
as the functional
W^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]
in which the substitution was performed
\{\Gamma_{\ldots}^{\ldots}\}\to\{\hat{\Gamma}_{\ldots}^{\ldots}\}
. As a result, a deformed connected action is constructed by the strongly connected action on the diagonal
W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]
. In this case, the solution
B_{d,\mu}^{a}
of the deformed quantum equation of motion

\frac{\delta W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]}{\delta B_{\mu}^{a}(x)}\Bigg|_{B=B_{d}}=0,

generally speaking, does not coincide with the solution
B_{f,\mu}^{a}
of the standard quantum equation. Nevertheless, the following relations are valid in both situations

\displaystyle W^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B_{f}]

\displaystyle=W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B_{f}],

\displaystyle\hat{W}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B_{d}]

\displaystyle=W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B_{d}].

At the same time, there is no equality of strongly connected parts in both situations. It should be noted here that from the point of view of the procedure for finding the extremum, the solution
B_{d,\mu}^{a}
is more natural, since it minimizes the functional rather than its part. Thus, forgetting about particular variations, the issue of renormalization narrows down to the functional
W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{reg}}[B]
, which transforms to
W^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{ren}}[B]
, and according to which
\hat{W}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{ren}}[B]
is constructed, taking into account the rules described above.

Despite the obvious gain in the complexity of renormalization, there are some open questions along the way. First, are the solutions of
B_{f,\mu}^{a}
and
B_{d,\mu}^{a}
very different? After all, in the absence of regularization, they must match. Therefore, it is unclear whether their difference is small in any sense (according to
\Lambda
). Secondly, it is not at all clear whether the new renormalized connected action has a formulation using a functional integral. In other words, is it possible to supplement (extend) the regularized classical action (
19
) in such a way that it reproduces
\hat{W}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{ren}}[B]
?

6
One loop: strong case

6.1
Scalar operator

Let us start with the determinant for the operator
\mathfrak{G}_{0}

[

and the scalar case in Section 4 of

\displaystyle\ln\det\big(\mathfrak{G}_{0}/\mathfrak{G}_{0}|_{e=B=0}\big)\big|_{\mathrm{reg.}}=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4\times k}}\mathrm{d}^{4}

\displaystyle z_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{k}\,\big(\mathfrak{M}_{0}^{ac_{1}}(z_{1})+\delta^{ac_{1}}\partial_{z_{1}^{\nu}}\partial_{z_{1}^{\nu}}\big)R_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{1}-z_{2})\times\ldots

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\big(\mathfrak{M}_{0}^{c_{k}a}(z_{k})+\delta^{c_{k}b}\partial_{z_{k}^{\nu}}\partial_{z_{k}^{\nu}}\big)R_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{k}-y)\big|_{y=z_{1}}.

The main task of this section consists of two calculations.

1.

Find the singular component for the case
\mathfrak{G}_{0}=G_{0}

2.

Find the linear contribution with respect to the field

to the singular component for

The first part.
It is convenient to divide this computational task into several parts. First, we write out all possible combinations appearing in expansion (
98
) for
\mathfrak{G}_{0}=G_{0}

\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)=R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)\eta(|x|,\sigma),

(99)

where
\eta(\,\cdot\,,\sigma)
is a monotonic smooth function that is equal to one on
[0,1/\sigma]
and equal to zero on the semi-infinite interval

Fourth degree.
Let us write out all the possible coefficients for the integral

\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{1}\,B_{\mu}^{ab}(z_{1})B_{\nu}^{bc}(z_{1})B_{\sigma}^{cd}(z_{1})B_{\rho}^{da}(z_{1}).

z_{1}

\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)
. By direct calculation, we can verify that it is possible for five combinations to appear as singular coefficients

(101)

Third degree.
Let us do similar calculations for the situation with the third degree of the background field. It is clear that in this case only logarithmic singularities can occur, and instead of the fourth degree of the background field, a derivative appears as well. The main functionality is

\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{1}\,\big(\partial_{z_{1}^{\mu}}B_{\nu}^{ac}(z_{1})\big)B_{\sigma}^{cd}(z_{1})B_{\rho}^{da}(z_{1}).

\displaystyle-2^{2}

Second degree.
In this case, not only logarithmic singularities are possible when two background fields and two derivatives appear, but also power-law contributions containing only two background fields without derivatives. Consider the first case, then for a combination of the form

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{1}\,\big(\partial_{z_{1}^{\mu}}B_{\nu}^{ac}(z_{1})\big)\big(\partial_{z_{1}^{\sigma}}B_{\rho}^{ac}(z_{1})\big)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}

After integration by parts, we come to the representation

, then using the asymptotic expressions

for auxiliary integrals

119
) is written as follows

-\frac{c_{2}L}{48\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,\big(\partial_{z^{\mu}}B_{\nu}^{a}(z)\big)\big(\partial_{z^{\mu}}B_{\nu}^{a}(z)-\partial_{z^{\nu}}B_{\mu}^{a}(z)\big).

(127)

Returning to the contribution with a power-law behavior, we get the answer in the form

\frac{c_{2}\Lambda^{2}}{2}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,B_{\nu}^{a}(z)B_{\nu}^{a}(z)\bigg)\bigg(\int_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\hat{R}_{0}^{1}(x)A_{0}(x)\hat{R}_{0}^{1}(x)-2\hat{R}_{0}^{1}(0)\bigg).

(128)

Next, we use the auxiliary notation from Section
7.8

\frac{\rho_{0}}{4\pi^{2}}=R_{0}^{1}(0),\,\,\,\frac{\rho_{3}}{4\pi^{2}}=\int_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,R_{0}^{1}(x)A_{0}^{\phantom{1}}(x)R_{0}^{1}(x).

(129)

Then, performing the final substitutions and summing up all the answers outlined above, we obtain a relation of the form

\ln\det\big(G_{0}/G_{0}|_{B=0}\big)\big|_{\mathrm{reg.}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}\frac{c_{2}\Lambda^{2}(\rho_{3}-2\rho_{0})}{8\pi^{2}}S_{2}[B]-\frac{c_{2}L}{96\pi^{2}}W_{-1}[B].

(130)

The second part.
The calculation of a contribution linear with respect to the field from the gauge condition can be greatly simplified. To do this, we first write out functional derivatives with respect to the background field
B_{\mu}^{a}
and to the field
e_{\mu}^{a}
for the corresponding determinant at the point
e_{\mu}^{a}=B_{\mu}^{a}
. Using formula (
46
) and performing a similar calculation, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta B_{\mu}^{g}(z)}\ln\det(\mathfrak{G}_{0}|_{\mathrm{reg.}})\bigg|_{e=B}

\displaystyle=-D_{\mu}^{cd}(z)G_{0}^{ad}|_{\mathrm{reg.}}(x,z)\big|_{x=z}f^{cga},

(131)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta e_{\mu}^{g}(z)}\ln\det(\mathfrak{G}_{0}|_{\mathrm{reg.}})\bigg|_{e=B}

\displaystyle=+D_{\mu}^{cd}(z)G_{0}^{da}|_{\mathrm{reg.}}(z,x)\big|_{x=z}f^{agc}.

(132)

Next, we make two important observations.

1.

First, their sum is equal to the variation
\ln\det(G_{0}|_{\mathrm{reg.}})
with respect to
B_{\mu}^{g}(z)
.

2.

Secondly, the functions are equal because of the symmetry of the kernel
G_{0}^{ab}(x,y)=G_{0}^{ba}(y,x)
.

Thus, calculating the variation for (
130
) and multiplying by
1/2
, we get the final answer in the form

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,e_{\mu}^{g}(z)\Bigg(\frac{\delta}{\delta e_{\mu}^{g}(z)}\ln\det(\mathfrak{G}_{0}|_{\mathrm{reg.}})\bigg|_{e=B}\Bigg)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}\frac{c_{2}\Lambda^{2}(\rho_{3}-2\rho_{0})}{8\pi^{2}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,e_{\nu}^{a}(z)B_{\nu}^{a}(z)\bigg)-\frac{c_{2}L}{48\pi^{2}}\Gamma_{1}[e].

(133)

6.2
Vector operator

Let us now consider the determinant for the regularized operator
\mathfrak{G}_{1}
. The perturbative formula for such an object is written out in the same way as

\displaystyle\ln\det\big(\mathfrak{G}_{1}/\mathfrak{G}_{1}|_{e=B=0}\big)\big|_{\mathrm{reg.}}=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4\times k}}\mathrm{d}^{4}

\displaystyle z_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{k}\,\big(\mathfrak{M}_{1\nu\mu_{1}}^{\,\,ac_{1}}(z_{1})+\delta^{ac_{1}}\delta_{\nu\mu_{1}}\partial_{z_{1}^{\nu}}\partial_{z_{1}^{\nu}}\big)R_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{1}-z_{2})\times

\displaystyle\ldots\times

\displaystyle\big(\mathfrak{M}_{1\mu_{k}\nu}^{\,\,c_{k}a}(z_{k})+\delta^{c_{k}b}\delta_{\mu_{k}\nu}\partial_{z_{k}^{\nu}}\partial_{z_{k}^{\nu}}\big)R_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{k}-y)\big|_{y=z_{1}}.

(134)

The main task of this section also consists of two calculations.

1.

Find the singular component for the case
\mathfrak{G}_{1}=G_{1}
.

2.

Find the linear contribution with respect to the field
e_{\mu}^{a}
to a singular component for
\mathfrak{G}_{1}
.

The first part.
To calculate it, it is convenient to use the previously obtained answer (
130
) for the scalar operator. Indeed, given the relation
F_{\mu\mu}^{a}(z)=0
, we obtain the following decomposition

\displaystyle\ln\det\big(G_{1}/G_{1}|_{B=0}\big)\big|_{\mathrm{reg.}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 4\ln\det\big(G_{0}/G_{0}|_{B=0}\big)\big|_{\mathrm{reg.}}

(135)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4\times 2}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{1}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{2}\,F_{\mu\nu}^{ac}(z_{1})R_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{1}-z_{2})F_{\nu\mu}^{ca}(z_{2})R_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{2}-z_{1}).

(136)

By shifting the variable in the second term, leaving only the singular part of the coefficient and using the result (
130
), we get the answer in the form

\ln\det\big(G_{1}/G_{1}|_{B=0}\big)\big|_{\mathrm{reg.}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}\frac{c_{2}\Lambda^{2}(\rho_{3}-2\rho_{0})}{2\pi^{2}}S_{2}[B]+\frac{5c_{2}L}{24\pi^{2}}W_{-1}.

(137)

The second part.
Using the definition of the operator from (
17
), we can write the formula for the first functional derivative

\frac{\delta}{\delta e_{\mu}^{g}(z)}\ln\det(\mathfrak{G}_{1}|_{\mathrm{reg.}})\bigg|_{e=B}=2f^{agc}D_{\nu}^{cd}(z)G_{1\nu\mu}^{\,\,da}(z,x)\big|_{x=z}.

(138)

Next, we note that it is convenient to use the decomposition of the Green’s function by degrees of the field strength tensor. In this case, only the following five terms can provide a singular contribution to the right hand side of (
138
)

\displaystyle 2f^{agc}D_{\mu}^{cd}(z)G_{0}^{da}(z,x)\big|_{x=z}

(139)

\displaystyle+\,

\displaystyle 8f^{agc}\partial_{z^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4\times 2}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{2}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{3}\,\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(z-z_{2})B_{\sigma}^{cd}(z_{2})\partial_{z_{2}^{\sigma}}\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{2}-z_{3})F_{\nu\mu}^{da}(z_{3})\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{3}-x)\big|_{x=z}

(140)

\displaystyle+\,

\displaystyle 8f^{agc}\partial_{z^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4\times 2}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{2}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{3}\,\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(z-z_{2})F_{\nu\mu}^{cd}(z_{2})\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{2}-z_{3})B_{\sigma}^{da}(z_{3})\partial_{z_{3}^{\sigma}}\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{3}-x)\big|_{x=z}

(141)

\displaystyle+\,

\displaystyle 4f^{agc}\partial_{z^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{2}\,\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(z-z_{2})F_{\nu\mu}^{ca}(z_{2})\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{2}-x)\big|_{x=z}

(142)

\displaystyle+\,

\displaystyle 4f^{agc}B_{\nu}^{cd}(z)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z_{2}\,\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(z-z_{2})F_{\nu\mu}^{da}(z_{2})\hat{R}_{0}^{\Lambda}(z_{2}-x)\big|_{x=z}.

(143)

Note that the first part is equal to the doubled variation of (
133
) with respect to the field
e_{\mu}^{a}
, while the remaining terms are calculated using previously used formulas, see auxiliary integrals for calculating coefficients with the functional (
100
). Putting all the parts together, we get the result

\frac{c_{2}\Lambda^{2}(\rho_{3}-2\rho_{0})}{4\pi^{2}}B_{\mu}^{a}(z)-\frac{5c_{2}L}{24\pi^{2}}D_{\nu}^{ga}(z)F_{\nu\mu}^{a}(z),

(144)

which leads to a final answer of the form

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,e_{\mu}^{g}(z)\Bigg(\frac{\delta}{\delta e_{\mu}^{g}(z)}\ln\det(\mathfrak{G}_{1}|_{\mathrm{reg.}})\bigg|_{e=B}\Bigg)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}\frac{c_{2}\Lambda^{2}(\rho_{3}-2\rho_{0})}{4\pi^{2}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,e_{\nu}^{a}(z)B_{\nu}^{a}(z)\bigg)+\frac{5c_{2}L}{24\pi^{2}}\Gamma_{1}[e].

(145)

7
Two loops: strong case

7.1
Decomposition of Green’s functions

Vector function.
For further calculations, it is convenient to use auxiliary decompositions for the Green’s function, both for the scalar
\mathfrak{G}_{0}^{\Lambda}
and for the vector
\mathfrak{G}_{1}^{\Lambda}
. Hereafter, the superscript
\Lambda
indicates the presence of regularization. Note that the field
e_{\mu}^{a}
, responsible for fixing the gauge condition, is chosen equal to the background field
B_{\mu}^{a}
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{G}_{0}^{\Lambda}\to G_{0}^{\Lambda}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{1}^{\Lambda}\to G_{1}^{\Lambda}
, see (
20
) and (
21
). So, we decompose the function
G_{1\mu\nu}^{\Lambda ab}(x,y)
with respect to the degrees of the field strength tensor with an allocation of singular components in a symmetrical form. Then we get

G_{1\mu\nu}^{\Lambda ab}(x,y)=\delta_{\mu\nu}G_{0}^{\Lambda ab}(x,y)+\mathcal{N}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x,y)+\mathcal{L}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x,y)+PS_{1\mu\nu}^{\,\,ab}(x,y),

(146)

where each part is determined based on a value of singularity and the degree of the field strength tensor. Let us explain the notation.

1) The contribution of
\delta_{\mu\nu}G_{0}^{\Lambda ab}
corresponds to the summand proportional to the zero degree of the field strength tensor
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}
, and thus is the Green’s function for the scalar operator.

2) The contribution of
\mathcal{N}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}
corresponds to the summand proportional to the first degree of the field strength tensor
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}
and containing the first three singular orders of the functional

2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,G_{0}^{\Lambda ac}(x,z)F_{\mu\nu}^{cd}(z)G_{0}^{\Lambda db}(z,y).

(147)

It is convenient to fix its explicit form as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\,\big(F_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)+F_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(y)\big)\theta(x-y)

(148)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\big(B_{\sigma}^{ac}(x)F_{\mu\nu}^{cb}(x)+F_{\mu\nu}^{ac}(y)B_{\sigma}^{cb}(y)\big)2\partial_{x^{\sigma}}\tau(x-y)+n_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x,y),

where auxiliary symmetric functions are defined by the equalities

\theta(x)=\int_{\mathrm{B}_{1/\sigma}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x-z)R_{0}^{\Lambda}(z),

(149)

\tau(x)=\int_{\mathrm{B}_{1/\sigma}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\int_{\mathrm{B}_{1/\sigma}}\mathrm{d}^{4}y\,\Big(R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x-z)R_{0}^{\Lambda}(z-y)R_{0}^{\Lambda}(y)-R_{0}^{\phantom{1}}(z)R_{0}^{\phantom{1}}(z-y)R_{0}^{\phantom{1}}(y)\Big).

(150)

In this case, the symmetric function
n_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x,y)
is constructed in a similar way using
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}
, as well as either two background fields or a derivative of the background field. In this case, it may be required that for
y\to x
in the main order, the function
n_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x,y)
should have the order
\ln(\Lambda/\sigma)/\Lambda^{2}
. Its explicit form is not important, since it does not lead to singular contributions due to the property
n_{\mu\mu}^{ab}(x,y)=0
.

3) The contribution of
\mathcal{L}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}
is equal to the main singular part of the term proportional to the second degree of the field strength tensor, and is equal to

\mathcal{L}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x,y)=2\big(F_{\mu\sigma}^{ac}(x)F_{\sigma\nu}^{cb}(x)+F_{\mu\sigma}^{ac}(y)F_{\sigma\nu}^{cb}(y)\big)\tau(x-y).

(151)

4) The contribution
PS_{1\mu\nu}^{\,\,ab}
contains all remaining parts. It has two finite derivatives and also depends on the boundary conditions. Considering the symmetry properties of all the previous parts, it can be argued that it has the property
PS_{1\mu\nu}^{\,\,ab}(x,y)=PS_{1\nu\mu}^{\,\,ba}(y,x)
.

Scalar function.
Similarly, we decompose the Green’s function for the ghost fields. However, only that part
52
52

52

Next decomposition orders must be additionally calculated.
of a singularity decomposition will be presented here, which is necessary for the tasks of this paper, see Section
1
. First, we note that the function
G_{0}^{\Lambda ab}(x,y)
can be divided into two parts

G_{0}^{\Lambda ab}(x,y)=G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{ab}(x,y)+PS_{0}^{ab}(x,y),

(152)

where the non-linear part of
PS_{0}
has two finite derivatives and is an analog of the function
PS_{1}
, while the first term has a local character, which can be described as follows: the function
G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{ab}(x,y)
is a finite sum, each term of which is equal to the product of a special function that does not depend on the background field and has the argument
x-y
by
p(x)+p(y)
, where
p
is a polynomial of finite degree with respect to the background field and its first three derivatives. It can be shown that in the first orders with respect to singularities, the function
G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{ab}(x,y)
can be decomposed as follows

\displaystyle G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{ab}(x,y)=\delta^{ab}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x-y)

\displaystyle+2B_{\mu}^{ab}(y)\partial_{x^{\mu}}\theta(x-y)

(153)

\displaystyle+\partial_{y^{\nu}}B_{\mu}^{ab}(y)\big(\delta_{\mu\nu}\theta(x-y)+2\kappa_{\nu\mu}(x-y)\big)

(154)

\displaystyle+B_{\mu}^{ac}(y)B_{\nu}^{cb}(y)\big(\delta_{\mu\nu}\theta(x-y)+4\partial_{x^{\mu}}\partial_{x^{\nu}}\tau(x-y)\big)+\ldots,

(155)

where an additional auxiliary function has the form

\kappa_{\nu\mu}(x)=\int_{\mathrm{B}_{1/\sigma}}\mathrm{d}^{4}z\,R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x-z)z^{\nu}\partial_{z^{\mu}}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(z).

(156)

Further, considering the symmetric combination, we obtain the relation

\displaystyle G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{ab}(x,y)=\delta^{ab}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x-y)

\displaystyle+\big(B_{\mu}^{ab}(x)+B_{\mu}^{ab}(y)\big)\partial_{x^{\mu}}\theta(x-y)

(157)

\displaystyle+B_{\mu}^{ac}(y)B_{\nu}^{cb}(y)\big(\delta_{\mu\nu}\theta(x-y)+4\partial_{x^{\mu}}\partial_{x^{\nu}}\tau(x-y)\big)+\ldots

In fact, within the framework of the decomposition, using symmetry was equivalent to replacing one type of singularity with another, that is,

\delta_{\mu\nu}\theta(x)+2\kappa_{\nu\mu}(x)\longleftrightarrow x^{\nu}\partial_{x^{\mu}}\theta(x),

(158)

which can be verified by integration by parts. Note that the term proportional to the first degree of the background field has a symmetrical appearance, while the fields for the third term are written out at only one point. This is due to the fact that the main purpose of this work is to study singular components, for which the proposed decomposition is sufficient. When trying to search for explicit
53
53

53

Recall that this paper describes a deviation from the covariant case. At the same time, a structure of singularities is proposed for a number of counter-terms without calculating explicit coefficients.
coefficients for the vertices of renormalization it also be necessary to represent the terms for the third and fourth degrees of the background field.

Let us perform

Such transformations are standard in the analysis of diagrams with one and two integration operators. They allow us to express the singularity explicitly. For more information, see

for an example.

and factor the individual terms into a singular part depending on the variable

. For convenience, we write out in the form of a table the decomposition by ”values” of the singularities of the function

.

D_{\sigma}(x)=\partial_{x^{\sigma}}+B_{\sigma}(y)+(x-y)^{\eta}\partial_{y^{\eta}}B_{\sigma}(y)+\ldots,

(160)

where the group indices have been omitted again for convenience. After a series of auxiliary calculations, we obtain the following summary table.

7.2
Singular operators

For example, consider an arbitrary smooth matrix-valued function of two variables
f^{ab}(x,y)
. We assume that it is symmetric, that is,
f^{ab}(x,y)=f^{ba}(y,x)
. The main task of this section is to calculate the singular part of the expansion with respect to the regularizing parameter
\Lambda
for the values

\mathrm{J}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[f]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}y\,\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)f^{ba}(y,x),

(162)

\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\rho}[f]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}y\,\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)f^{ba}(y,x),

(163)

where the kernels of integro-differential operators are given by the equalities

\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)=f^{acd}\Big(D_{\sigma}^{ch}(x)D_{\rho}^{gf}(y)G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{hf}(x,y)\Big)G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{de}(x,y)f^{egb},

(164)

\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)=f^{hcd}G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{cg}(x,y)G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{de}(x,y)f^{egf}D_{\sigma}^{ha}(x)D_{\rho}^{fb}(y).

(165)

Taking into account the definition for the auxiliary number
\rho_{3}
from Section (
236
), the answers are written as follows

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{\sigma\rho}[f]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta_{\sigma\rho}\Lambda^{2}c_{2}\rho_{3}}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,f^{aa}(x,x)

(166)

\displaystyle+\frac{Lc_{2}}{96\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big(\big(M_{1\rho\sigma}^{ba}(x)+4D_{\rho}^{bc}(x)D_{\sigma}^{ca}(x)\big)f^{ab}(x,y)\Big)\Big|_{y=x},

\displaystyle\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\rho}[f]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle+\frac{Lc_{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big(\big(D_{\rho}^{bc}(x)D_{\sigma}^{ca}(x)\big)f^{ab}(x,y)\Big)\Big|_{y=x}.

(167)

Let us first prove the first formulated relation. To do this, in integral (
162
), we shift the variable
x\to x+y
and replace the integration area as follows
\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathrm{B}_{1/\sigma}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Next, we decompose the densities in such a way that all the parts that depend on the background field have the variable
y
, and the parts containing a singular density contain only
x
. In this way, the desired factorization can be obtained for each term. Note that the Green’s functions have already been redefined as necessary, see the tables in (
159
) and (
161
) in Section
7.1
. In tern, the test function has a standard Taylor series expansion of the form

f^{ba}(y,x+y)=f^{ba}(y,y)+x^{\nu}\partial_{z^{\nu}}f^{ba}(y,z)\big|_{z=y}+\frac{x^{\nu}x^{\mu}}{2}\partial_{z^{\nu}}\partial_{z^{\mu}}f^{ba}(y,z)\big|_{z=y}+\ldots

(168)

Next, the analysis method is reduced to sorting through all possible combinations. Here are some supporting comments. For convenience, we will use the ”value of singularity” shown in tables (
159
) and (
161
).

1) The Green’s functions
DDG_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
G_{\mathrm{loc}}
have a total value of singularity of
r^{-6}
. There is only one such option, see the left columns of tables (
159
) and (
161
). In this case, the operator from (
164
) is converted to the form

\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x+y,y)\to f^{acd}\Big(-\delta^{cg}\partial_{x^{\sigma}}^{\phantom{1}}\partial_{x^{\rho}}^{\phantom{1}}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)\Big)\delta^{de}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)f^{egb}=c_{2}\delta^{ab}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)\partial_{x^{\sigma}}^{\phantom{1}}\partial_{x^{\rho}}^{\phantom{1}}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x).

(169)

Then we write out the interesting parts of the test function. It is clear that the degree of the variable
x
must be even, otherwise the integral will be zeroed due to symmetry properties, see (
115
). Considering the fact that the total value of the singularity for the density should be
r^{-4}
or stronger, we choose only two terms

f^{ba}(y,x+y)\to f^{ba}(y,y)+\frac{x^{\nu}x^{\mu}}{2}\partial_{z^{\nu}}\partial_{z^{\mu}}f^{ba}(y,z)\big|_{z=y}.

(170)

Next, after substituting the obtained decompositions and using auxiliary formulas from the first part of Section
7.8
, we come to the answer

-\frac{\delta_{\sigma\rho}\Lambda^{2}c_{2}\rho_{3}}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,f^{aa}(x,x)+\frac{Lc_{2}}{96\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big(\big(\delta_{\sigma\rho}A_{0}(x)+4\partial_{x^{\rho}}\partial_{x^{\sigma}}\big)f^{aa}(x,y)\Big)\Big|_{y=x}.

(171)

2) The Green’s functions
DDG_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
G_{\mathrm{loc}}
have a total value of singularity of
r^{-5}
. According to the table, there can be four such options. In this case, the operator from (
164
) can be converted to the following form

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x+y,y)\to

\displaystyle\frac{c_{2}B_{\mu}^{ba}(y)}{2}\bigg[\Big(-\partial_{x^{\sigma}}^{\phantom{1}}\partial_{x^{\rho}}^{\phantom{1}}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)\Big)\Big(2\partial_{x^{\mu}}^{\phantom{1}}\theta(x)\Big)+\Big(-2\partial_{x^{\sigma}}^{\phantom{1}}\partial_{x^{\rho}}^{\phantom{1}}\partial_{x^{\mu}}^{\phantom{1}}\theta(x)\Big)R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)\bigg]

(172)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{c_{2}B_{\sigma}^{ba}(y)}{2}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)\partial_{x^{\rho}}^{\phantom{1}}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)-\frac{c_{2}B_{\rho}^{ba}(y)}{2}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)\partial_{x^{\sigma}}^{\phantom{1}}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x).

(173)

In this case, it is necessary to take only one term of the first degree from the test function

f^{ba}(y,x+y)\to x^{\nu}\partial_{z^{\nu}}f^{ba}(y,z)\big|_{z=y}.

(174)

After substituting and using the formulas from Section
7.8
, we arrive at an answer of the form

\frac{Lc_{2}}{96\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big(\big(-2\delta_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}B_{\mu}^{ba}(x)\partial_{x^{\mu}}^{\phantom{1}}+4B_{\sigma}^{ba}(x)\partial_{x^{\rho}}^{\phantom{1}}+4B_{\rho}^{ba}(x)\partial_{x^{\sigma}}^{\phantom{1}}\big)f^{ab}(x,y)\Big)\Big|_{y=x}.

(175)

3) The Green’s functions
DDG_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
G_{\mathrm{loc}}
have a total value of singularity of
r^{-4}
. Taking into account the decompositions from the tables, a total of 13 terms are obtained. At the same time, only the main term must be saved for the test function. Omitting the routine calculations that are obtained taking into account the auxiliary integrals from Section
7.8
, we write only the final answer

\frac{Lc_{2}}{96\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big(-\delta_{\sigma\rho}B_{\mu}^{bc}(x)B_{\mu}^{ca}(x)+2B_{\rho}^{bc}(x)B_{\sigma}^{ca}(x)+2B_{\sigma}^{bc}(x)B_{\rho}^{ca}(x)\Big)f^{ab}(x,x).

(176)

Next, note that we can add an arbitrary number of derivatives of the background fields, since the convolution of symmetric
f^{ab}
with antisymmetric
\partial B^{ba}
leads to zero. Summing up all the answers and adding the missing number of
\partial B
, we get the stated answer (
166
). Relation (
167
) is obtained after preserving the main order for all functions and integrating them by parts. Both formulas are proven.

7.3
Nonlocal part

Let us study the singular nonlocal part appearing in the two-loop approximation of the quantum action. The main method is to sort through all possible combinations, taking into account the auxiliary decompositions for the Green’s functions from (
146
) and (
157
). In this case, the subsections will be named taking into account the parts of the Green’s functions involved in the decomposition. For example, the two-loop part of
\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(\Gamma_{3}^{2})
consists of diagrams with three Green’s functions. Then, using the expansion from (
146
), the contribution of
G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{1}
will be called all singular terms in diagrams obtained from
\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(\Gamma_{3}^{2})
by replacing two Green’s functions with
G_{\mathrm{loc}}
and one Green’s function with
PS_{1}
in all possible ways. Similar designations can be used for the rest of the parts. Here are all combinations that can lead to nonlocal singularities:

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{3}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\to G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{1},\,\,\,G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{0},\,\,\,G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N}PS_{1},

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Omega_{3}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\to G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{1},\,\,\,G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{0},

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{4}\big)

\displaystyle\to G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{1},\,\,\,G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{0},\,\,\,\mathcal{N}PS_{1}.

The remaining combinations do not contain singularities from ultraviolet divergences. This fact is easily verified by counting ”degrees” near the diagonal.

7.3.1
Part
G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{i}
for
\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(\Gamma_{3}^{2})

The calculations in this section are based on the use of singular integrals from Section
7.2
. Let us start with the situation
i=1
, since
i=0
is a special case. To do this, we recall that by construction, the part
PS_{1\mu\nu}^{\,\,ab}
has two finite derivatives and therefore can be used as a test function. However, it is necessary to explain how the search should be performed. Suppose there is a two-loop diagram, then we need to cut one of the three lines and then connect the function
PS_{1\mu\nu}^{\,\,ab}
to the cut point. In this case, the remaining two Green’s functions should be replaced by
G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{ab}
. Thus, one diagram will turn into three, and
\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(\Gamma_{3}^{2})
, consisting of 6 diagrams, will turn into 18 components.

Mathematically, this can be rewritten as follows. First, we write out all strongly connected vertices that can be obtained by connecting two vertices
\Gamma_{3}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{2}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{3}^{2}\big)=

\displaystyle{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}-{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}+2{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}-2{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}-{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}+2{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}-2{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}-{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}+2{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}-2{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}.

Next, replace
G_{1\mu\nu}^{\Lambda ab}
with
\delta_{\mu\nu}G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{ab}
and the resulting differential operator in the kernel notate by
r_{\sigma\rho}^{\,ab}(x,y)
, so we have

\mathbb{H}_{2}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{3}^{2}\big)\Big|_{G_{1}^{\Lambda}\to G_{\mathrm{loc}}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}y\,r_{\sigma\rho}^{\,ab}(x,y)a_{\sigma}^{a}(x)a_{\rho}^{b}(y).

Then a contribution in
G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{1}
we are interested is the singular part of the integral

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}y\,r_{\sigma\rho}^{\,ab}(x,y)PS_{1\sigma\rho}^{\,\,ab}(x,y).

(177)

For convenience, we write out the density for each individual part after the appropriate replacement. We get 12 transitions in total:

{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to-4\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)+2\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y),\,\,\,\,\,\,{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to-\mathcal{R}_{\rho\sigma}^{ab}(x,y)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\rho\sigma}^{ab}(x,y),

(178)

{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to 4\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y),\phantom{-+2\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)}\,\,\,\,\,\,{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to\delta_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}\mathcal{R}_{\mu\mu}^{ab}(x,y),

(179)

{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y),\phantom{-4+2\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)}\,\,\,\,\,\,{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\rho\sigma}^{ab}(x,y),

(180)

{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to-\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to\delta_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\mu\mu}^{ab}(x,y),

(181)

{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y),\phantom{-\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\mu\mu}^{ab}(x,y),

(182)

{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y),\phantom{-\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\rho\sigma}^{ab}(x,y).

(183)

Summarizing the expressions obtained, we arrive at the relation for the operator kernel of interest

\displaystyle r_{\sigma\rho}^{\,ab}(x,y)=

\displaystyle-4\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)-\mathcal{R}_{\rho\sigma}^{ab}(x,y)-\delta_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}\mathcal{R}_{\mu\mu}^{ab}(x,y)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)+\frac{5}{2}\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\rho\sigma}^{ab}(x,y)-2\delta_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\mu\mu}^{ab}(x,y).

Therefore, taking into account the definitions of (
162
) and (
163
), the integral (
177
) is rewritten as the sum of six functionals

-4\mathrm{J}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\sigma\rho}]-\mathrm{J}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\rho\sigma}]-\mathrm{J}_{\sigma\sigma}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\rho\rho}]+\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\sigma\rho}]+\frac{5}{2}\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\rho\sigma}]-2\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\sigma}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\rho\rho}].

(184)

Additionally, we note that similar expressions can be written out for each individual diagram. The representations for the corresponding integrals have the form:

\displaystyle\longrightarrow-\mathrm{J}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\rho\sigma}]-\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\sigma\rho}]+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\rho\sigma}],

\displaystyle-{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow-4\mathrm{J}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\sigma\rho}]-\mathrm{J}_{\sigma\sigma}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\rho\rho}]-\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\sigma}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\rho\rho}],

\displaystyle\longrightarrow-4\mathrm{J}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\sigma\rho}]+2\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\sigma\rho}]-\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\sigma}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\rho\rho}],

\displaystyle-2{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 2\mathrm{J}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\sigma\rho}]+\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\rho\sigma}],

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 2\mathrm{J}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\sigma\rho}]+\mathrm{\hat{J}}_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}[PS_{1\rho\sigma}].

Summing up the last relations, we can check the consistency with the representation of (
184
). Let us use the obtained values (
166
) and (
167
) for singular parts, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{3}^{2}\big)\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\,\frac{9\Lambda^{2}c_{2}\rho_{3}}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,PS_{1\rho\rho}^{\,\,aa}(x,x)

(185)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\frac{Lc_{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\bigg(\bigg[-\frac{5}{48}M_{1\rho\sigma}^{\,\,ba}(x)+\frac{\delta_{\sigma\rho}}{2}M_{0}^{ba}(x)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\frac{11}{24}D_{\rho}^{bc}(x)D_{\sigma}^{ca}(x)-\frac{5}{8}F_{\rho\sigma}^{ba}(x)\bigg]PS_{1\sigma\rho}^{\,\,ab}(x,y)\bigg)\bigg|_{y=x}.

By construction,
PS_{0}
has the same smoothness properties as the function
PS_{1}
. Therefore, we can use the resulting formula (
185
) by substituting the function
\delta_{\sigma\rho}^{\phantom{1}}PS_{0}^{ab}(x,y)
instead of
PS_{1\sigma\rho}^{\,\,ab}(x,y)
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{3}^{2}\big)\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\,\frac{9\Lambda^{2}c_{2}\rho_{3}}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,PS_{0}^{aa}(x,x)

(186)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\frac{27Lc_{2}}{48\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big(M_{0}^{ba}(x)PS_{0}^{ab}(x,y)\Big)\Big|_{y=x}.

7.3.2
Part
G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N}PS_{1}
for
\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(\Gamma_{3}^{2})

In this case, the counting process is similar to the previous one. Let us explain the differences and complete the calculations. Note that now, after the stage of cutting the line in a two-loop diagram from
\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{3}^{2}\big)
, one of the two remaining Green’s functions
G_{1}^{\Lambda}
is replaced by
G_{\mathrm{loc}}
, and the second one is for
\mathcal{N}
, and vice versa. So the result is not 18 diagrams, but 36.

Next, note that in the main order near the diagonal, when
x\sim y
, the function
\mathcal{N}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x,y)
is proportional to
2F_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(y)\theta(x-y)
, therefore, for a singular contribution to occur, both derivatives must be inside the loop. Thus, of the sum of
\mathbb{H}_{2}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{3}^{2}\big)
, only the following terms can eventually give a non-zero contribution

{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}-{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}+2{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}-2{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}+{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}-{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}.

Note that the derivative can only be shifted inside the loop, since a derivative acting on the tail leads to a finite contribution. Therefore, we proceed to

-2{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}+4{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}+{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}-{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}.

Now, replacing as follows

G_{1\mu\nu}^{\,\,ab}(x,y)\to\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\phantom{1}}\delta^{ab}R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x-y),\,\,\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x,y)\to 2F_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(y)\theta(x-y),

note that the first diagram leads to zero due to
F_{\mu\mu}^{ab}(y)=0
. The remaining parts, after shifting the variable
x\to x+y
, add up to a density of the form

-2c_{2}F_{\mu\rho}^{ba}(y)\Big(R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)\partial_{x^{\sigma}}\partial_{x^{\mu}}\theta(x)\Big)-4c_{2}F_{\rho\mu}^{ba}(y)\Big(R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)\partial_{x^{\sigma}}\partial_{x^{\mu}}\theta(x)\Big)+c_{2}F_{\sigma\rho}^{ba}(y)\Big(R_{0}^{\Lambda}(x)\partial_{x^{\mu}}\partial_{x^{\mu}}\theta(x)\Big).

Next, multiplying by
PS_{1\sigma\rho}^{\,\,ab}(y,y)
and using the auxiliary relations from Section
7.8
, we get the following answer

\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{3}^{2}\big)\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N}PS_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}\frac{3Lc_{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}y\,\Big(F_{\rho\sigma}^{ba}(y)PS_{1\sigma\rho}^{\,\,ab}(y,y)\Big).

(187)

To complete the picture, we note that the expressions for each individual diagram are written as follows:

3{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N}PS_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}-\frac{3}{2}{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N}PS_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{3}^{2}\big)\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N}PS_{1}},

{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N}PS_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N}PS_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N}PS_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}0.

7.3.3
Parts for
\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(\Omega_{3}^{2})

Let us consider all three variants of the emerging nonlocal singularities in turn. First, let us recall the explicit form of the two-loop diagram

\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Omega_{3}^{2}\big)=-{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}.

Its special feature is that it contains only one Green’s function
G_{1}^{\Lambda}
. The other two are equal to
G_{0}^{\Lambda}
. This fact leads to some changes in the computing process.

1) Consider the case of
G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{1}
. Since the function
PS_{1}
is present only in the middle line, it is it that should be cut. The resulting diagram is similar to the one already studied and is reproduced as follows

-\frac{1}{4}{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\to\mathcal{R}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y)-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\sigma\rho}^{ab}(x,y).

(188)

Therefore, using (
166
) and (
167
), we get the answer

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Omega_{3}^{2}\big)\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\Lambda^{2}c_{2}\rho_{3}}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,PS_{1\rho\rho}^{\,\,aa}(x,x)

(189)

\displaystyle+\frac{Lc_{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\bigg(\bigg[\frac{1}{48}M_{1\rho\sigma}^{\,\,ba}(x)-\frac{1}{24}D_{\rho}^{bc}(x)D_{\sigma}^{ca}(x)\bigg]PS_{1\sigma\rho}^{\,\,ab}(x,y)\bigg)\bigg|_{y=x}.

2) Consider the case of
G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{0}
. It is divided into two parts, since
PS_{0}
is contained in both the midline and Green’s scalar functions. In the first case, the answer is obtained by replacing the function
PS_{1\sigma\rho}
in (
189
) with
\delta_{\sigma\rho}PS_{0}
, that is

-\frac{\Lambda^{2}c_{2}\rho_{3}}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,PS_{0}^{aa}(x,x)+\frac{Lc_{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big(M_{0}^{ba}(x)PS_{0}^{ab}(x,y)\Big)\bigg|_{y=x}.

In the second case, the side line is cut, and the resulting diagram is reduced to the one already studied, for which the transition is valid

\frac{1}{4}{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\bigg|_{\sigma=\rho}\to\mathcal{\hat{R}}_{\mu\mu}^{ab}(x,y).

Then, integrating with function
PS_{0}^{ab}(x,y)
, we get the result in the form

-\frac{Lc_{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big(M_{0}^{ba}(x)PS_{0}^{ab}(x,y)\Big)\bigg|_{y=x}.

Considering that there are two side lines, after summing the results, we get

\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Omega_{3}^{2}\big)\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}-\frac{\Lambda^{2}c_{2}\rho_{3}}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,PS_{0}^{aa}(x,x)-\frac{3Lc_{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big(M_{0}^{ba}(x)PS_{0}^{ab}(x,y)\Big)\bigg|_{y=x}.

(190)

7.3.4
Parts for
\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(\Gamma_{4})

In this case, the contribution from the quartic vertex consists of three diagrams. Let us recall their explicit form

{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}-{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}+{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}.

They consist of two Green’s functions on the diagonal, so first it is convenient to figure out exactly which singularities occur in the function
G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{ab}(x,x)
. Using the representation form (
157
), we see that only the main order
\delta^{ab}\Lambda^{2}R_{0}^{1}(0)
is singular on the diagonal, since the remaining terms, which previously led to singularities during integration, in the case of
y=x
are reduced due to the properties

\partial_{x^{\mu}}\theta(x-y)\big|_{y=x}=0,\,\,\,\delta_{\mu\nu}\theta(0)+4\partial_{x^{\mu}}\partial_{x^{\nu}}\tau(x-y)\big|_{y=x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}0.

(191)

Therefore, when calculating nonlocal components, we can use the substitution

G_{\mathrm{loc}}^{ab}(x,x)\to\delta^{ab}\Lambda^{2}R_{0}^{1}(0).

1) Consider the cases with
G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{i}
. Let us immediately note that due to the symmetry of
\delta^{ab}
, the third diagram in this case gives zero contribution. Further, summing up the spatial indexes, we note that

\frac{1}{4}{\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\bigg|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{1}}={\centering\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{}}}}}}$}\@add@centering}\bigg|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{1}}=-2c_{2}\Lambda^{2}R_{0}^{1}(0)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,PS_{1\rho\rho}^{\,\,aa}(x,x).

Thus, the relation is valid

\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{4}\big)\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}6c_{2}\Lambda^{2}R_{0}^{1}(0)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,PS_{1\rho\rho}^{\,\,aa}(x,x).

(192)

Next, replacing
PS_{1\mu\nu}
with
\delta_{\mu\nu}PS_{0}
, we get a similar equality

\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{4}\big)\Big|_{G_{\mathrm{loc}}PS_{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}24c_{2}\Lambda^{2}R_{0}^{1}(0)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,PS_{0}^{aa}(x,x).

(193)

2) Consider the case of
\mathcal{N}PS_{1}
. It can be immediately simplified by replacing
\mathcal{N}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x,x)
with the main order
2F_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)\theta(0)
, and also by throwing out the second diagram, since the trace of the field strength tensor is zero. Next, recall that
\theta(0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}L/(8\pi^{2})
, see Section
7.8
, then we get

\displaystyle\to\frac{Lc_{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,F_{\rho\sigma}^{ba}(x)PS_{1\sigma\rho}^{\,\,ab}(x,x),

\displaystyle\to\frac{Lc_{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,F_{\rho\sigma}^{ba}(x)PS_{1\sigma\rho}^{\,\,ab}(x,x).

Thus, after summing up, we get an answer of the form

\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{4}\big)\Big|_{\mathcal{N}PS_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{s.p.}}}{{=}}\frac{3Lc_{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,F_{\rho\sigma}^{ba}(x)PS_{1\sigma\rho}^{\,\,ab}(x,x).

(194)

7.4
Local part

Once again, we use the general idea of counting singular components, proposed in Section
7.3
. To do this, first, using the decompositions from (
146
) and (
153
), we write out a list of combinations that can lead to nonzero singular components:

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{3}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\to G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N},\,\,\,G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N},\,\,\,G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{L},\,\,\,G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Omega_{3}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\to G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N},\,\,\,G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{L},\,\,\,G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}\big(\Gamma_{4}\big)

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N},\,\,\,G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{L},\,\,\,G_{\mathrm{loc}}G_{\mathrm{loc}}.

The remaining combinations do not lead to singularities of ”ultraviolet” nature.

7.4.1
Part
G_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathcal{N}\mathcal{N}
for
\mathbb{H}_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}(\Gamma_{3}^{2})

Let us immediately note that the main order of the Green’s function
G_{0}^{\Lambda}
has the order of the singularity proportional
r^{-2}
, which is due to the behavior near the diagonal. Further, the main order of the function
\mathcal{N}
